‘Hunger Games: Sunrise On The Reaping’ To Shine Brightly Around The World With $160M-$190M WW Debut – Preview

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

With the domestic box office headed to $9 billion year-to-date by Thanksgiving, it’s been sleepy at the fall box office when it comes to worldwide event films, but that’s about to change with the sixth Hunger Games movie, The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping which looks to create a greenhouse effect at planet Earth’s movie theaters with a $160M to $190M worldwide opening.

That’s a big return to fortune for the Lionsgate Suzanne Collins franchise which hit a low point with 2024’s The Hunger Games: Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes which opened to $44.6M domestic, $100.8M worldwide stemming from complications from the actors’ strike (which ended shortly before the pic’s opening) as well as the novel being the least favorite among fans.

Presales stateside for the fifth Francis Lawrence-directed title in the franchise remain relentless, and well ahead of both Wicked movies which respectively debuted to $112.5M and $147M in 2024 and 2025. The concern with any female driven feature is that it will be front loaded, hence, the domestic swing at 3,900 theaters (which includes all PLFs and Imax, before some of the latter’s portion goes to AMC for Netflix’s The Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth) is between $90M-$120M. NRG is sitting on an $80M+ opening, however most of exhibition sees this movie well north of $100M. One source with keen knowledge on presales tells Deadline, “Yeah, this is definitely a three-digit opening with a one in front of it.” Watch on Deadline

Even after the Thanksgiving shave of AMC’s Imax, Sunrise on the Reaping will still have a big Imax footprint with Regal, Cinemark, Harkins, Marcus, etc.

Even if Sunrise on the Reaping comes in at $90M, or worse $80M, it’s still a win for Lionsgate, not to mention it’s not often that we see a franchise’s sixth or nth movie surging in its domestic opening from the previous installment’s start, so bravo there.

The continued vibrant demo here for is women 17-34. Women under 25 in first choice is massive, -9% behind Wicked at the same point in time, and 100% ahead of The Devil Wears Prada 2 ($76.7M). Women over 25 are the second strongest with their first choice -20% behind Wicked and -23% behind Devil Wears Prada 2.

Previews start today at 6:30pm in PLFs followed by a 12noon preview showtime tomorrow for the PG-13 movie which is the prequel story about Woody Harrelson’s character Haymitch Abernathy (played in Sunrise by fresh face actor Joseph Zada), who was a mentor to Jennifer Lawrence’s Katniss Everdeen and Josh Hutcherson’s Peeta Mellrk in the original movie. Sunrise on the Reaping also follows the younger days of Effie Trinket (played by Elle Fanning in this film, Elizabeth Banks in the earlier pics), Caesar Flickerman (Kieran Culkin taking over for Stanley Tucci), Plutarch (Jesse Plemons here, the character portrayed by the late Philip Seymour Hoffman) and President Snow (Ralph Fiennes, played previously by the late Donald Sutherland, and by Tom Blyth in The Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes).

Sunrise on the Reaping will see its rays touch upon 84 countries in roughly 43,000 screens worldwide and 39,000 screens internationally. The pic will be everywhere expect Japan, and including China. Hunger Games movies have historically launched day-and-date in north of 80 some odd territories. In reported box office, the 2013 second Lawrence movie, Catching Fire, holds the record opening with $304M ($158M overseas), followed by 2014’s Mockingjay – Part 1 with $276.2M ($154.3M overseas), Mockingjay 2‘s $241.M ($144.5M offshore debut), followed by the original 2012 movie which minted $214.2M global opening ($61.7M offshore). The international opening floor here for Sunrise on the Reaping is $70M+. The feature take of the Collins IP has rallied in most of Europe and Latin America, but softer in Asian territories. We’re hearing hot presales in UK, Mexico and Brazil.

Elsewhere stateside, Amazon MGM Studio’s Sylvester Stallone biopic I Play Rocky is going wide at 2,000 after two weekends of limited play, the pic from 2x Oscar winner Peter Farrelly counting $1M through yesterday off 87% Rotten Tomatoes certified fresh ratings and 98% with moviegoers. The pic, which follows how Stallone bet on himself to make Rocky, is looking at a $12M start in North America.

Warner Bros. TV & Paramount TV Studios To Merge With Channing Dungey & Matt Thunell As Presidents

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Just like Paramount Television Studios’ first iteration, PTVS 2.0’s run as a standalone label was short-lived.

A month after the completion of Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery’s merger into Skydance, two of the combined company’s three television studios, Warner Bros. Television Group and Paramount Television Studios, are being consolidated into one. The merger, planned for early 2027, comes 15 months after FTVS was resurrected following Skydance’s acquisition of Paramount.

The new entity, Warner Bros. Television Studios, will be co-run by WBTV’s Channing Dungey and PTVS’ Matt Thunell who have been named Presidents. They will continue to report to George Cheeks, Co-Chair and Chief Content Officer of Skydance TV. As previously announced, Dungey also serves as Chair of Skydance Global Cable Media, which combines the cable network portfolios of Warner Bros. Discovery and Paramount, featuring more than 50 brands. Related Stories Executives Skydance Cable Shakeup: Betsy Sanner Ayala, Jason Sarlanis, Tina Perry & Michael Ouweleen To Oversee Programming; Howard Lee Exiting Executives Louis Carr Leaving As BET President Amid Post-Merger Skydance TV Restructuring

There is no mention of layoffs in the studio restructuring announcement though cuts are inevitable when divisions are merged; it was also the case when PTVS 1.0 was folded into Skydance’s other TV studio, CBS Studios, in 2024, a move also overseen by Cheeks at the time. In his memo announcing the new combined studio, which you can read below, Cheeks alludes to the pending cuts, noting that “HR will share more information as plans are finalized.” Watch on Deadline

In smaller changes, BET Studios, which a year ago was moved to CBS Studios, is on the move again, this time relocating to Warner Bros. Television Studios. And Skydance Sports Entertainment, which was announced by Cheeks as part of the Skydance TV Studios Group last month “with details to follow,” will be housed at CBS Studios, which is led by President David Stapf.

Merging WBTV and PTVS is not surprising — putting WBTV, CBS Studios and PTVS in one one Skydance TV Studios unit under Cheeks positioned all three for future consolidation. The manner in which it happened is.

Skydance is doing the consolidation on similar time table as Disney, which folded Touchstone TV into 20th Television pretty quickly after the acquisition of Fox assets. (It took four years for the final step, merging the remaining two TV studios, 20th Television and ABC Signature.)

As Deadline previously noted, of the three Skydance TV studios, CBS Studios has the most clearly defined lane as the primary supplier to CBS (as well as home of Star Trek and an opportunistic third-party seller).

Meanwhile, there was significant overlap between Warner Bros Television and PTVS. They each had been tasked with mining the IP of their sister film studios and have been main suppliers to their sibling streamers HBO Max and Paramount+, respectively. Their common playing area is the open streaming market, as both are actively selling outside and have hits on multiple platforms.

With the Paramount and Warner Bros. movie studios under joint leadership and HBO Max and Paramount+ bound to merge into one, combining the two TV studios appeared inevitable — and was rumored even before the Paramount-WBD transaction was completed.

Making Dungey and Thunell equal co-leaders of the combined studio is raising eyebrows given that they are at different points of their career, with Dungey coming into the partnership with a lot more experience and a stature as one of the top, widely respected television executives in town.

Meanwhile, the well-liked Thunell was a rising star at David Ellison’s Skydance where he was President of TV, and his upward trajectory has continued at Paramount Skydance and now at post-WBD merger Skydance.

Given their different backgrounds, Thunell reporting to Dungey would not have been farfetched; that scenario was shut down when Cheeks announced Skydance TV Studios, with Channing, Thunell and Stapf all reporting to him.

Thunell taking over WBTV also had been rumored though not feasible right away due to the massive size of that studio’s operation and slate that dwarf PTVS. That may still be endgame as it’s unclear how long the current co-head structure will be in place. It would give Thunell time to get to know WBTV, working alongside studio veteran Dungey.

While Dungey is taking on additional responsibilities by adding the Paramount cable brands to her WBD cable portfolio, her sharing the reins of WBTV and reporting to Cheeks instead of Skydance’s CEO as was the case at WBD is noteworthy as the Hollywood glass ceiling has been increasingly coming into play over the past year or so with some high-profile departures of female executives, most recently Paramount’s Cindy Holland and Netflix’s Bela Bajaria, and the Disney CEO job going to Josh D’Amaro and not Dana Walden.

The new Warner Bros. Television Studios will be home to more than 120 active television series, including Harry Potter and The Philosopher’s Stone (HBO), The Pitt (HBO Max), Ted Lasso (Apple TV), Georgie & Mandy’s First Marriage (CBS), Reacher (Prime Video), Marshals (CBS), Landman (Paramount+), Emily in Paris (Netflix) and the upcoming Neuromancer (Apple TV).

“Bringing together two of the industry’s most prolific television studios creates a formidable creative engine, uniting extraordinary talent, resources, acclaimed programming and iconic franchises with the global scale to compete at the highest level. With Channing and Matt at the helm, we have two exceptional leaders who understand what it takes to empower creative talent, foster bold ideas and deliver programming that resonates with audiences everywhere,” Cheeks said. “Alongside CBS Studios, led by the incomparable David Stapf, Warner Bros. Television Studios gives us two world-class studios, with the breadth, depth and creative ambition to deliver for Skydance platforms and third-party partners across the industry.”

Said Dungey: “Matt has extraordinary creative instincts, and a deep understanding of great storytelling. I couldn’t ask for a better partner as we bring these storied studios together. We have a unique opportunity to create something truly special for our creative partners and audiences around the world. I’m very excited to begin this new chapter.”

Said Thunell: “Channing is one of the most accomplished and respected executives in our industry, with an extraordinary track record of building successful businesses, developing enduring partnerships and delivering some of television’s most acclaimed programming. Ever since our time together at Netflix, I’ve hoped we’d have the opportunity to work together again, and I’m thrilled that day has finally come. We’re bringing an unmatched roster of talent under one roof, building on the remarkable legacies of two storied studios. The possibilities ahead are extraordinary, and I couldn’t be more excited to partner with Channing to champion the ambitions of our creative talent and bring exceptional stories to audiences around the world.”

Team,Today, I’m sharing an important next step for how our television studios will serve the creative community. We are combining the Warner Bros. Television Group and Paramount Television Studios into one studio, which will operate as Warner Bros. Television Studios, beginning in early 2027. Until then, both studios will continue to operate independently.This move aligns our leadership, resources and creative focus for two studios with considerable success producing for streaming, as well as broadcast. By bringing these teams together, we can build on their strengths and create an even stronger home for great talent and great storytelling.Channing Dungey and Matt Thunell will be Presidents of Warner Bros. Television Studios, jointly leading the combined studio and reporting to me, with oversight of its creative departments and an expansive and growing portfolio of scripted and unscripted programming. Channing and Matt will also oversee BET Studios and Warner Bros. International Television Production studios around the world.David Stapf continues in his role as President of CBS Studios, reporting to me, building on the studio’s strength as one of the industry’s leading global studios. Home to many of the Network’s biggest franchises and hits, David and his team remain a critical supplier to our broadcast business while continuing to grow their streaming footprint. CBS Studios International, Skydance Sports Entertainment and See It Now Studios will operate under the CBS Studios umbrella.Collectively, Warner Bros. Television Studios and CBS Studios present two powerful creative engines with extraordinary talent, award-winning programming, hit franchises and the global scale to serve Skydance platforms and third-party partners across the industry. Add to that, CBS Entertainment, led by Amy Reisenbach, and the Global Cable Media team under Channing, Skydance TV is a creative force in entertainment across scripted, unscripted and live events.What Happens NextChanning, Matt and David will be connecting with teams in the coming weeks to talk more about the new structure. We have extraordinary creative talent, an unmatched portfolio and tremendous opportunity ahead. Alaska Airlines Eyes New Long-Haul Boeing 787 Route: But There's A Major Twist

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Alaska Airlines is keen to fly to Lisbon, which is the capital of Portugal and an important destination for tourism. The oneworld member has previously expressed interest in flying there. A major ongoing stumbling block is the lack of slots, at least until, and if, Lisbon's new airport opens in many years.

As Ishrion Aviation has pointed out, Alaska Airlines applied for slots for a daily service to Lisbon. Unsurprisingly, it was not granted any of them. Slots do exist, but only in limited numbers and at particular times. It is likely that the growing US operator would either have to reduce its request substantially (say, to a logical three weekly flights) or accept inferior times, as WestJet did. Given Alaska Airlines' hub structure, a poor schedule would not be sensible.

The carrier has big plans for its long-haul network, although it is finding the going tough for now. Recently, it called its long-haul routes 'a drag', but they'd always take time to develop, even before the rise in the price of fuel. Against this backdrop, it'd be reasonable to question the appeal of potential future flights between Seattle, obviously Alaska Airlines' primary hub, and the Portuguese capital.

In the 12 months to September 2026, booking data via OAG shows that 22,700 round-trip passengers flew indirectly between the two cities. Obviously, that volume is small, although nonstop flights, along with hopefully strong promotions, would easily grow that by 40% to 50% in the first season.

Nonetheless, the supposed daily frequency would be highly excessive (three weekly flights would make much more sense). As such, it would therefore rely even more than usual on lower-yielding transfer traffic via Seattle.

Despite the low existing traffic, Lisbon is Seattle's third-largest unserved European market, excluding places like Athens, which Alaska Airlines has already announced. Zurich was the top unserved market (36,100 passengers), which stopped having nonstop flights with the Swiss leisure operator Edelweiss in 2025, followed by the oneworld hub of Madrid (25,500).

Of course, traffic (both existing and likely stimulated) is just one part of it. Across all airlines, cabins, and passengers, the average fare to Lisbon was $641 one-way (excluding a fuel surcharge, taxes, and add-ons). That was similar to Barcelona, to which Delta Air Lines began flying this year, almost certainly to get in before Alaska Airlines announced the route. However, Lisbon flights would cover less distance and be less expensive to serve.

The airline's international expansion continues.

More notably, and perhaps worryingly, Seattle-Athens had an average one-way fare of $646, which was less than 1% higher than Lisbon's. However, flights to the capital of Greece, which Alaska Airlines will begin on May 12, 2027, will cover 19% more distance and be much more expensive to operate. Of course, in these instances, and elsewhere where it is the only operator, it should get a reasonable price premium for the convenience of flying nonstop.

The carrier's debut to Europe took place in April 2026, when the first flight from Seattle to Rome took off. The launch came after Alaska Airlines began its first-ever long-haul service, which was to Asia. The first route was from Washington State to Tokyo Narita in May 2025, followed by Seoul Incheon four months later.

Alaska Airlines' Planned Operations To Europe: July 2027

Faces British Airways, Delta, and Virgin Atlantic

After Rome came London Heathrow (May 2026), followed by Reykjavík (May 2026). Next year, Alaska Airlines will begin flying to Athens (May 2027) and Paris CDG (May 2027). When writing, the carrier currently plans five transatlantic routes, with a big emphasis on leisure markets, just like WestJet from Canada to Europe. This is perhaps why Alaska Airlines has not announced flights to Madrid, in what would be a logical tie-up with fellow oneworld member Iberia.

Let's narrow the focus to mid-July 2027. In that week, Alaska Airlines will be one of 12 airlines flying from Seattle to Europe, which will collectively have 147 weekly departures (21 daily). Discover Airlines will be there, as the Lufthansa Group's leisure unit is replacing Lufthansa itself. Alaska Airlines will have 28 weekly departures (four daily) and rank second behind Delta (35 weekly; five daily).

A Bear Market Is Coming Eventually. History Says This Is the Best Investing Move You Can Make.

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A bear market is coming eventually, but history suggests the bigger risk to your wealth is how you react when it arrives.

By John Ballard – Nov 18, 2026 at 1:15PM EST

Bear markets are a regular part of investing. Since 1928, the S&P 500 has fallen into one -- a drop of at least 20% from a recent high -- about every 3.5 years, on average, according to Hartford Funds and Ned Davis Research. The typical bear market has erased about 35% of the index's value.

That sounds frightening, but bear markets are usually short. The average one has lasted about 9.6 months. These downturns are less a disaster than they are the cost of admission to building long-term wealth. The bigger risk is how investors react when the next one arrives.

For anyone with many years until retirement, here's why I'd stay fully invested.

According to Hartford and Ned Davis, the S&P 500 has had 28 bull markets and 27 bear markets since 1928. Through all those ups and downs, it delivered a compound annual return of about 10%, according to The Motley Fool's research. The market rises more in a bull market than it loses in a bear market.

It pays to keep investing when things look worst. Capital Group tracked the 18 biggest market declines since the Great Depression and found the S&P 500 was higher five years later every time. MFS found that after the 10 worst calendar years since 1975, a $100,000 investment was worth about $207,000 on average five years later.

Don't think that you'll be better off waiting to invest until the market moves higher. In fact, history says you'll be wealthier by staying fully invested through every down year.

J.P. Morgan Asset Management found that $10,000 invested in the S&P 500 at the start of 2006 grew to $80,619 by the end of 2025 for someone who stayed fully invested. However, missing just the 10 best days cut that to $35,866.

Take 2025: The S&P 500 fell nearly 19% in the spring, yet its best day came on April 9, in the middle of the turmoil. Missing that one day would have cut the year's 17.9% return to 7.6%.

Now, some will think: "I'll just sit in cash and wait until a down year to buy bargains and make a fortune." That's not a smart strategy either. Bear markets are infrequent. Over the past 95 years, stocks have been rising about 78% of the time, according to Hartford.

Bear markets are also hard to predict. A normal pullback of 10% or so can easily be mistaken for the start of a bigger decline.

Waiting for the perfect moment tends to backfire. Morningstar found that the average dollar in U.S. mutual funds and exchange-traded funds (ETFs) earned 8.7% a year over the decade ended 2025, versus 9.9% for the funds themselves, largely because of when investors bought and sold.

A bear market is, indeed, coming, but we don't know when. The best move is to stay invested and not sell when the market goes down, because missing the biggest up days after the bottom will cost you a lot more money down the road than if you just stayed invested.

Time in the market is far more important than market timing. From 1970 through September 2026, every 20-year rolling period for the S&P 500 produced a gain, according to Mellon data published by Ave Maria Mutual Funds.

Even the worst timing can still make money for patient investors. Hartford calculated that investing $10,000 at the peak just before each of the six bear markets since 1995 would have still turned $60,000 into $364,774 by the end of 2025. That beats staying in cash and earning almost nothing.

As Warren Buffett wrote in his 1991 shareholder letter, "The stock market serves as a relocation center at which money is moved from the active to the patient."

The best time to start is always when you have money available. Pick a low-cost index fund such as the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -0.10%), which tracks the S&P 500, or the Vanguard Total Stock Market ETF (VTI -0.02%), which covers the whole U.S. market.

Set up an automatic purchase of a fixed amount each month or with each paycheck, and keep it running no matter what happens in the markets. Keep money you'll need in the next few years out of stocks, and you'll be on your way to growing your net worth.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 794%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 203% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

Who are Mauricio Pochettino’s assistant coaches at Manchester United?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

Manchester United parted ways with Michael Carrick after a disastrous start to the Premier League season with just one win from 10 games.

Manchester United have spent a reported 12 million to bring Mauricio Pochettino and his coaching team from the United States to Old Trafford, with the Argentine determined to work alongside several familiar faces.

United paid around $10 million to secure Pochettino’s release from the US team and with a further $2 million reportedly required to secure members of his backroom staff.

The 54-year-old has built a close working relationship with his coaching team throughout spells at Espanyol, Southampton, Tottenham, Paris Saint-Germain and Chelsea. Several of those staff members have followed him to Manchester United.

Jesus Perez is Pochettino’s long-time assistant and is widely regarded as his right-hand man. Their relationship began at Espanyol, where Perez initially worked as an analyst before becoming his assistant at Southampton. He has followed the Argentine throughout his career with spells at Tottenham, PSG and USA. He is now set to take the same role at Old Trafford.

Jimenez will work as United’s goalkeeping coach. He first joined Pochettino’s coaching setup at Espanyol in 2011 and has remained alongside him throughout his managerial career, including their time in England, France and the United States.

The manager’s 31-year-old son has also joined the coaching group. Sebastiano started working with his father during their time at Tottenham and has continued as a sports scientist and fitness coach with him with the USA team. He is also joining Pochettino at Manchester United.

The former Argentine defender has worked with Pochettino since his first managerial job at Espanyol. He has been involved in training sessions, video analysis and tactical preparation, helping the manager develop game plans.

The arrivals mean a major change behind the scenes at Carrington following the departures of Michael Carrick and his coaching staff.

Mauricio Pochettino is set to face Liverpool in his first game as Manchester United head coach on Sunday.

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

Warner Bros. TV, Paramount TV Studios to Combine as a Single Mega-Studio

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Channing Dungey and Matt Thunell will lead the combined studio, while CBS Studios remains separate under David Stapf.

Skydance is making a big move with its TV studios, combining Warner Bros. Television and Paramount Television Studios into a single entity that will have more than 100 productions under its umbrella.

The combined studio will be known as Warner Bros. Television Studios, a nod to the larger of the two current units. The new organization will officially launch early next year, with each group operating as usual until then. WBTV chief Channing Dungey and PTVS head Matt Thunell will be presidents of the combined studio, reporting to Skydance TV co-chair and chief content officer George Cheeks. Dungey also continues to lead Skydance’s cable portfolio.

CBS Studios, headed by David Stapf, will keep operating as its own entity. CBS Studios International, Skydance Sports Entertainment and nonfiction unit See It Now Studios fall under CBS Studios. BET Studios will become part of Warner Bros. TV Studios.

“Bringing together two of the industry’s most prolific television studios creates a formidable creative engine, uniting extraordinary talent, resources, acclaimed programming and iconic franchises with the global scale to compete at the highest level,” Cheeks said in a statement. “With Channing and Matt at the helm, we have two exceptional leaders who understand what it takes to empower creative talent, foster bold ideas and deliver programming that resonates with audiences everywhere. Alongside CBS Studios, led by the incomparable David Stapf, Warner Bros. Television Studios gives us two world-class studios, with the breadth, depth and creative ambition to deliver for Skydance platforms and third-party partners across the industry.”

Dungey and Thunell — who previously worked together at Netflix — offered praise for their soon-to-be partnership in statements of their own.

“Matt has extraordinary creative instincts, and a deep understanding of great storytelling. I couldn’t ask for a better partner as we bring these storied studios together,” said Dungey. “We have a unique opportunity to create something truly special for our creative partners and audiences around the world. I’m very excited to begin this new chapter.”

Added Thunell, “Channing is one of the most accomplished and respected executives in our industry, with an extraordinary track record of building successful businesses, developing enduring partnerships and delivering some of television’s most acclaimed programming. Ever since our time together at Netflix, I’ve hoped we’d have the opportunity to work together again, and I’m thrilled that day has finally come.”

Skydance combining the two TV studios follows a similar playbook to other big media mergers in the past decade. After Disney took over 21st Century Fox’s entertainment assets, four TV studios eventually became three, then two and now just one (20th Television). A year before its own 2025 merger with Skydance, Paramount shut down Paramount TV Studios, then revived the name under as it combined Skydance TV and Showtime/MTV Entertainment under Thunell.

It’s also worth noting that the antitrust lawsuit settlement that paved the way for the Paramount-Warner Bros. Discovery merger did not explicitly mention TV studio operations, even as it set benchmarks for the number of feature film releases from the Warner Bros. and Paramount movie studios. The primary TV provisions in the settlement concern carriage agreements with cable and satellite operators and the editorial independence of CBS News and CNN. Even though the combined Warner Bros. TV Studios and CBS Studios produce some 150 series, including international co-productions, that side of the business went unremarked on.

The combination of the two studios will also likely result in layoffs. “We know there will be questions about what this means for individual teams,” Cheeks wrote in a memo to staff Wednesday (read it in full, below). “HR will share more information as plans are finalized.” It will also mean one fewer place for writers and producers to pitch and sell series projects.

The text of Cheeks’ memo outlining the changes is below.

Team,Today, I’m sharing an important next step for how our television studios will serve the creative community. We are combining the Warner Bros. Television Group and Paramount Television Studios into one studio, which will operate as Warner Bros. Television Studios, beginning in early 2027. Until then, both studios will continue to operate independently.This move aligns our leadership, resources and creative focus for two studios with considerable success producing for streaming, as well as broadcast. By bringing these teams together, we can build on their strengths and create an even stronger home for great talent and great storytelling.Channing Dungey and Matt Thunell will be Presidents of Warner Bros. Television Studios, jointly leading the combined studio and reporting to me, with oversight of its creative departments and an expansive and growing portfolio of scripted and unscripted programming. They are exceptional creative leaders who understand how to support talent, champion bold ideas and build strong partnerships across our industry. Channing and Matt will also oversee BET Studios and Warner Bros. International Television Production studios around the world.David Stapf continues in his role as President of CBS Studios, reporting to me, building on the studio’s strength as one of the industry’s leading global studios. Home to many of the Network’s biggest franchises and hits, David and his team remain a critical supplier to our broadcast business while continuing to grow their streaming footprint. CBS Studios International, Skydance Sports Entertainment and See It Now Studios will operate under the CBS Studios umbrella.Collectively, Warner Bros. Television Studios and CBS Studios present two powerful creative engines with extraordinary talent, award-winning programming, hit franchises and the global scale to serve Skydance platforms and third-party partners across the industry. Add to that, CBS Entertainment, led by Amy Reisenbach, and the Global Cable Media team under Channing, Skydance TV is a creative force in entertainment across scripted, unscripted and live events.What Happens NextChanning, Matt and David will be connecting with teams in the coming weeks to talk more about the new structure. We know there will be questions about what this means for individual teams. HR will share more information as plans are finalized.I’m excited about what we’re building. We have extraordinary creative talent, an unmatched portfolio and tremendous opportunity ahead. Bringing these teams together gives us an even stronger foundation to support storytellers, champion great ideas and bring exceptional television to audiences around the world.George

Skydance to Fold Paramount TV Studios Into Warner Bros. TV Under Channing Dungey and Matt Thunell

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CBS Studios will remain standalone production unit under David Stapf

Skydance is officially combining Warner Bros. Television Group and Paramount Television Studios, two of its key TV production units, Variety has learned.

The new combined entity will be known as Warner Bros. Television Studios, with Channing Dungey and Matt Thunell serving as presidents. They will report into George Cheeks, co-chair and chief content officer of Skydance TV. Cheeks made the announcement Wednesday morning in an internal memo to staff, which can be read in full below.

Dungey and Thunell will also oversee BET Studios and Warner Bros. International Television Production, while it was previously announced that Dungey also now serves as chair of Skydance Global Cable Media, which combines the cable network portfolios of Warner Bros. Discovery and Paramount.

The merger of the two studios will be completed in early 2027.

Meanwhile, CBS Studios will continue to operate as a standalone entity under David Stapf, also reporting to Cheeks. CBS Studios International, Skydance Sports Entertainment and See It Now Studios will operate under the CBS Studios umbrella.

“Bringing together two of the industry’s most prolific television studios creates a formidable creative engine, uniting extraordinary talent, resources, acclaimed programming and iconic franchises with the global scale to compete at the highest level,” Cheeks said. “With Channing and Matt at the helm, we have two exceptional leaders who understand what it takes to empower creative talent, foster bold ideas and deliver programming that resonates with audiences everywhere. Alongside CBS Studios, led by the incomparable David Stapf, Warner Bros. Television Studios gives us two world-class studios, with the breadth, depth and creative ambition to deliver for Skydance platforms and third-party partners across the industry.”

It had previously been speculated that PTVS would be merged back into CBS Studios as it had been before. But the move to combine PTVS into one of the other studio units is not surprising. WBTV is a major powerhouse, with its current slate across a number of networks and streamers including shows like “The Pitt,” “Ted Lasso,” “Shrinking,” “Georgie & Mandy’s First Marriage,” “Abbott Elementary,” and the upcoming “Harry Potter” HBO series. Related Stories WWE, Disney Team for 'Avengers: Doomsday'-Themed Worlds Collide, John Cena Classic MS NOW Has 2026 Midterm Elections Coverage: Here's How to Watch Election Night Results Live Online Free

“Matt has extraordinary creative instincts, and a deep understanding of great storytelling,” Dungey said. “I couldn’t ask for a better partner as we bring these storied studios together. “We have a unique opportunity to create something truly special for our creative partners and audiences around the world. I’m very excited to begin this new chapter.”

Paramount Television Studios currently produces or co-produces shows such as “Reacher” at Prime Video and Taylor Sheridan’s Paramount+ shows, including “Landman,” “Tulsa King,” “Dutton Ranch” and more. However, PTVS has been shut down and revived multiple times under various names, with Skydance only bringing the division back in its current form in 2025 upon the completion of the Skydance-Paramount merger.

“Channing is one of the most accomplished and respected executives in our industry, with an extraordinary track record of building successful businesses, developing enduring partnerships and delivering some of television’s most acclaimed programming,” Thunell said. “Ever since our time together at Netflix, I’ve hoped we’d have the opportunity to work together again, and I’m thrilled that day has finally come. We’re bringing an unmatched roster of talent under one roof, building on the remarkable legacies of two storied studios. The possibilities ahead are extraordinary, and I couldn’t be more excited to partner with Channing to champion the ambitions of our creative talent and bring exceptional stories to audiences around the world.”

CBS Studios, meanwhile, continues to produce a long string of hit shows under Stapf and his team. The studio still has the CBS cornerstone “NCIS” on the air in its 24th season, with the franchise currently encompassing the prequel “NCIS: Origins” and “NCIS: New York.” CBS Studios also co-produces “Criminal Minds,” which aired for 15 seasons on CBS and was revived for an additional five at Paramount+.

The studio also has shows like the “FBI” franchise, the hit comedy “Ghosts,” “Elsbeth,” the “Fire Country” franchise, and “Little House on the Prairie” at Netflix. Meanwhile, consistent hitmakers Robert and Michelle King, who have called CBS Studios home for more than two decades, just launched to legal drama “Cupertino” on CBS via CBS Studios.

This marks the latest moves under the combined Skydance-Warner Bros. Discovery under David Ellision. In addition to the previously announced cable networks reorganization, it was reported earlier this week that Skydance Motion Picture Group co-chairs Dana Goldberg and Josh Greenstein have set their leadership team.

Co-Chair & Chief Content Officer, Skydance TV

Today, I’m sharing an important next step for how our television studios will serve the creative community. We are combining the Warner Bros. Television Group and Paramount Television Studios into one studio, which will operate as Warner Bros. Television Studios, beginning in early 2027. This move aligns our leadership, resources and creative focus for two studios with considerable success producing for streaming, as well as broadcast. By bringing these teams together, we can build on their strengths and create an even stronger home for great talent and great storytelling.

David Stapf continues in his role as President of CBS Studios, reporting to me, building on the studio’s strength as one of the industry’s leading global studios. Home to many of the Network’s biggest franchises and hits, David and his team remain a critical supplier to our broadcast business while continuing to grow their streaming footprint. CBS Studios International, Skydance Sports Entertainment and See It Now Studios will operate under the CBS Studios umbrella.

Collectively, Warner Bros. Television Studios and CBS Studios present two powerful creative engines with extraordinary talent, award-winning programming, hit franchises and the global scale to serve Skydance platforms and third-party partners across the industry. Darren Aronofsky’s Protozoa and Memory to Adapt Chris Miller’s ‘Chip War’ into Feature Documentary (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chris Miller’s book “Chip War: The Fight for the World’s Most Critical Technology” is being adapted into a feature documentary from Darren Aronofsky‘s Protozoa and Memory, Variety can reveal exclusively. “All Light, Everywhere” filmmaker Theo Anthony will direct the film.

Producers include Riel Roch-Decter and Anthony for Memory, and Aronofsky, Dylan Golden and Brendan Naylor for Protozoa. Executive producers are Jessica Harrop for Sandbox Films, Jenny Raskin for Impact Partners and Rachel Crane for Cranium Productions. CAA Media Finance, which arranged financing for the film, represents its worldwide distribution rights. Related Stories ‘Musk’ Doc Gets International Release Dates Skydance Sports Chief Gives Top Roles to Jeff Gerttula, Craig Barry

According to an official description, the “Chip War” doc will explore “the semiconductor industry, the vast and largely invisible supply chain that makes the chips inside every phone, weapon and AI model, along with the escalating struggle between the United States and China to control it. Amid political upheaval, corporate scandal and a wave of upcoming AI IPOs, the race for chips has never been more urgent.”

“The story of silicon and the people who shaped and are shaping it is the story of our time. When I read Chris’ brilliant book, I knew it had to find more audiences with moving images. Who better to bring it to life than the absolute original filmmaking of Theo Anthony?” Aronofsky said.

Anthony made his feature debut in 2016 with “Rat Film,” an experimental doc about the rat infestation in Baltimore, which helps tell a bigger story about classism and racism in the city. He followed that project with his 2019 “30 for 30” film “Subject to Review,” chronicling the rise of the instant replay system in professional tennis. His last documentary, “All Light, Everywhere,” premiered at Sundance 2021 and won the special jury prize for nonfiction experimentation.

Anthony, Memory and Protozoa are represented by CAA.

Shuckin' Shack Communities Rally to Turn the Tide Against Blood Cancer With a Record Breaking $123,805.16 Raised

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The 18-unit seafood franchise has capped off its fifth year of events supporting Blood Cancer United with record-breaking numbers, Franchise Partner-led initiatives and a deep community impact.

WILMINGTON, N.C., Nov. 18, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Following a month-long blitz dedicated to supporting local patients and blood cancer research, Shuckin' Shack Oyster Bar, the 18-unit seafood restaurant franchise, has wrapped up its fifth annual campaign supporting Blood Cancer United. The campaign spanned the entire month of September, Blood Cancer Awareness Month, with Franchise Partners driving tailor-made initiatives and offers at local Shacks as part of a shared, systemwide goal to Turn the Tide against blood cancer.

This year's campaign was the most successful one yet, raising a record breaking $123,805 through in-store events, online donations, merchandise sales and community outreach. The month-long effort culminated in a Sept. 26 One Day, One Cause finale, where Shacks throughout the system hosted events to cap off the month and celebrate the change made by the Franchise Partners, team members and guests who rallied around the cause.

"Giving each location the ability to do their own thing â create offers and events that they knew their communities and guests would love â resulted in great buy-in throughout the system," said Co-Founder Matt Piccinin. "Sept. 26 was a great day for One Day, One Cause. The Carolina Beach location, for example, had its best one-day event in five years, bringing in $44,000 on that Saturday alone."

The record-breaking campaign puts the 5 year annual total over $400,000 raised, all thanks to local Shack communities who stepped up in the fight against blood cancer. Systemwide, Franchise Partners brought their own creative energy to the month, hosting everything from beach parties to "paint and sip" nights where guests decorated holiday oyster shells. In Frederick, Maryland, Franchise Partner Eric Weller, whose mother passed away from leukemia, united his staff and community around the cause and drove nearly $22,000 in digital donations. Throughout the month, people shared their stories and memories of loved ones on Shuckin' Shack's socials and in the Shacks they visited.

"For one month, in our Shack communities, everyone is getting together for one cause," said Darren Keeler, Vice President of Marketing and Creative. "During One Day, One Cause, we saw a lot of repeat visitors, including people who are currently going through treatment. It's really positive to see them show up consistently for the past five years. Their family members will fly in for these events; their groups just keep growing and growing."

Family members of patients who had recently passed away and previously enjoyed attending Shuckin' Shack's benefit events were also in attendance. Keeler explained that, for one family, it was especially emotional as it was their first year without their loved one at the event. "It was heartwarming because they know that Blood Cancer United's research added days to her life, and this was another way for them to honor and celebrate her," he said.

"When we talk about community, this is what it's about," said CEO Jonathan Weathington. "We have our own ties with Blood Cancer United through Matt, Eric's mom and several others in our system. Cancer touches everyone. Turn The Tide is what actual community looks like; it's not a scenario where we're forcing people's hands and making a big scene. There may be some publicity that comes from it, but the heart of it is altruistic."

Shuckin' Shack will continue to build on this record momentum. With a Turn the Tide campaign already scheduled for next year, the brand remains committed to deepening its community roots, empowering its Franchise Partners and raising the bar each September in the fight against blood cancer.

Offering a "lifestyle experience," Shuckin' Shack Oyster Bar creates an environment that encourages guests to grab a seat, relax and take their taste buds on a trip to the coast; after all, life is too short for bland drinks and boring bars! The original Carolina Beach, NC Shack, which was just 900 square feet, was launched in 2007 with a plan to become the go-to local hangout for people of all ages. A place where friends and family can enjoy fresh, delicious meals, cold beer and craft cocktails in an environment that exudes fun and relaxation, Shuckin' Shack offers exceptional seafood and creative drinks that will make guests question why they ever settled for ordinary bar offerings.

Thanks to its bold flavors and impeccable vibes, Shuckin' Shack has continued to grow. In 2014, the brand began franchising and has since expanded to 18 units across six states. To learn more about Shuckin' Shack, visit http://www.theshuckinshack.com.

Sources: Rangers have rejected ‘big’ Djeidi Gassama offers as ‘only way’ for exit revealed

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Transfer Correspondent AUTHORITY Former Sky Sports and ESPN FC correspondent who specialises in global recruitment, contract negotiations, and high-velocity transfer breaking news. FOCUS Exclusive reporting, domestic and international transfer markets, and elite-level insights. THE INSIGHT Pete utilises a Global Network of Industry Sources to deliver verified, first-to-market reporting. His “Deep-Lens” analysis connects the dots between boardroom strategy and pitch-level recruitment, providing a level of transfer clarity that goes beyond surface-level rumours.

Rangers have no intention of cashing in on winger Djeidi Gassama in January amid interest from Monza, sources have told Football Insider.

The Italian side are the latest club to take an interest in the 23-year-old, but Derek McInnes has no doubt that he is not for sale.

Rangers are understood to have turned down “big offers” for the former Sheffield Wednesday man in previous transfer windows, and still view him as a key part of their long-term plans.

Sources say that the “only way” they would let Gassama out of the door in January is if an offer came in that was too good for them to refuse.

However, that is not expected to come from Monza, who are struggling at the foot of the Serie A table at the moment.

Rangers fully expect that Gassama will still be part of their squad in the second half of the season, and they are happy for that scenario to play out.

Gassama endured an inconsistent start to life at Ibrox last term, but still managed to notch nine goals and five assists in all competitions.

He already has three assists in the Premiership so far this term, and is currently winning the battle for regular minutes in Rangers’ wide areas.

McInnes is blessed with a wealth of options on the flanks, with Gassama, Findlay Curtis, Minsu Kim, Badredine Bouanani and Daisuke Yokota all pushing for starts.

However, it is Gassama who is emerging at the top of that group at the moment and appears to be winning the boss over with his performances.

That has reduced any chance of him being sold even further in recent weeks, with McInnes viewing him as a potential difference-maker for his team.

Football Insider revealed on Wednesday that Dinamo Bucharest winger Danny Armstrong is keen to return to Scotland and join Rangers in January.

The 29-year-old is flourishing in Romania, and already has 16 goal involvements from just 15 games in the top flight this season.

Sources say that Armstrong would find it “hard to turn down” a move to Ibrox this winter, and is keen to reunite with McInnes.

He previously played under the Gers boss at Kilmarnock, where he became a firm favourite of the manager.

16 Ecuadorian fishermen sue US government, allege drone attack destroyed their boat near the Galápagos

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A Sunday strike against a low-profile vessel that intelligence showed was participating in "narco-trafficking," killing two people on board, according to U.S. Southern Command. (Credit: U.S. SOUTHCOM)

More than a dozen Ecuadorian fishermen have sued the federal government, alleging they were wrongly targeted in a drone strike that destroyed their boat in the Pacific Ocean earlier this year as part of the Trump administration’s broader campaign against vessels suspected of drug trafficking.

The 16 fishermen aboard La Negra Francisca Duarte II say drones launched from a U.S. vessel struck their boat March 17, 2026, as they headed home from a two-week fishing trip near the Galápagos.

The strike set the boat ablaze, forcing the crew to jump overboard and escape in lifeboats, according to a lawsuit filed Monday in federal court in Pennsylvania. All 16 survived, but two suffered serious burns. One man’s leg was burned to the bone, the lawsuit says.

The fishermen’s lawyers say the crew had no ties to drug cartels or terrorist groups and posed no imminent threat when U.S. drones struck their vessel, which they say had been cleared by Ecuadorian authorities hours earlier.

This still image from Sept. 19, 2026, shows the moment before a projectile hits a vessel in the Caribbean the U.S. military alleged was involved in drug trafficking. (US Southern Command/Handout via Reuters)

US MILITARY LAUNCHES FIRST AUTONOMOUS WARFARE COMMAND TO DEPLOY UNMANNED SYSTEMS ACROSS LATIN AMERICA

After their boat was destroyed, the fishermen sailed toward a blue vessel, which the lawsuit identified as a U.S. ship. Some personnel wore U.S. Coast Guard vests, the suit says. They allegedly zip-tied the uninjured men, hooded them and forced them to stand barefoot on the hot metal deck with no food and little water.

One injured fisherman was taken below deck for treatment, where U.S. personnel allegedly told him they had carried out the drone strike and laughed about destroying the boat, according to the lawsuit.

The fishermen were held until the next day before they were put on an El Salvadoran ship that took them back to El Salvador, where they were held at a migrant center in "deplorable conditions" until the end of March.

Demonstrators gather to protest against the deportation of immigrants to El Salvador outside the Permanent Mission of El Salvador to the United Nations on April 24, 2025, in New York City. (Michael M. Santiago/Getty Images)

THE UNLIKELY SOUTH AMERICAN ALLY HELPING LEAD TRUMP'S DRUG CARTEL CRACKDOWN

The Department of War and the U.S. Coast Guard have denied knowing about or being involved in the March 17 attack on La Negra Francisca Duarte II.

U.S. Southern Command declined to comment, citing its policy of not issuing statements on pending or ongoing litigation.

A suspected drug-trafficking vessel erupts in flames following a U.S. military strike in the Eastern Pacific on June 18, 2026. (U.S. Southern Command)

The lawsuit argues that the Trump administration’s strikes in the Caribbean and Pacific violate federal and international law because they have been carried out outside an armed conflict. It adds that even if an armed conflict existed, the attacks could constitute war crimes under the Geneva Conventions.

The plaintiffs are seeking compensatory and other financial damages to be determined at trial, plus an injunction ordering the U.S. to end similar boat attacks.

11 unresolved mysteries FROM must answer in 5th and final season

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

From the town's origins to red lightning, 11 open FROM mysteries the final season must settle, and which ones the ending may not need.

Viewers of "From" have spent four seasons watching people trapped in a town nobody can leave, under rules nobody can explain, and each season has raised new questions faster than it has answered the old ones. Those questions now have a deadline. The series has been renewed for a fifth season that will be its last, ScreenRant reports, and that season is expected in 2027, according to Entertainment Weekly. After a long wait, the writers will have a single season to explain every unexplained rule, creature and power, or to decide to leave it alone. The open questions reach well beyond how the town began and into how it works from one night to the next. The number of them, more than any single riddle, is what keeps the ending uncertain.

The show will end at the length its makers first proposed, though that outcome was not settled along the way. ScreenRant reports that the series was pitched to MGM+ as five seasons and 50 episodes. In 2023, during the show's second season, executive producer Jeff Pinkner was weighing four, five or six seasons, according to Arizona Daily Star. The fifth and final season brings the series back to its original plan after a period when that plan was one option among several. Pinkner's view at the time was that when a series is built around its characters, the people making it can sense when it should stop. The makers will measure the ending by where the characters arrive, not by how many puzzles get closed. A fan's checklist and the finale may not match, and some threads may be answered only as far as the characters need them to be. That gap sets the fairest test for any open question: whether the ending depends on it, not whether it is intriguing.

FandomWire set out to find which open puzzles the show must solve to conclude its final season, from the red lightning to Julie's storywalking, the Boy in White and the Lake of Tears. Those puzzles are less separate than they look. In the fourth season's finale, the writers brought many of them into a single night and tied them to the same antagonist, so answering one may answer several. Some have already been partly settled on screen, while others grew more urgent after the town lost its protections, and "unresolved" means something different from one question to the next. Each slide that follows weighs what a question still hides and why the ending needs it answered.

Most of the show's riddles concern events inside Fromville, but this one concerns the town itself, and every other answer depends on it. Before the series ends, it has to say who made the town, what ties it to the world beyond and what becomes of the place and everything in it if the cycle ends, according to FandomWire. Those are the most basic facts about any place, and the show has yet to supply any of them. A finale can leave a side story unresolved, but a finale that never says what the town is would leave every other explanation without a basis. This is also the answer most likely to settle several questions at once, because the identity of the town's maker and the nature of its tie to the outside world would bear on nearly every mystery that grew out of them.

The confusion the residents feel is what drives the drama, and the people making the show treat that confusion as something to protect. Harold Perrineau, the actor who plays one of the town's leaders, has chosen not to find out how the series ends. "The torture (my character) is feeling is easier for me to embody if I really don’t know," Perrineau told the Arizona Daily Star. That gives the final season a harder job than delivering an explanation, since revealing what the town is will also end the uncertainty behind much of the characters' suffering.

The thing to watch, then, is not only who built Fromville but whether the show's makers reveal it in a way that gives that suffering a purpose rather than simply ending it.

The bargain behind Fromville is still one of the show's most basic open questions, and the final season will have to say who made it and how it works. FandomWire counts the sacrifice among the unanswered questions about the town's origins that the final season has to take up. Part of the trade is known: The original townspeople gave up seven children and received immortality in return, according to ComicBook.com. The town's founding was therefore a deliberate atrocity by a whole community, and any moral reckoning in the last season has to answer for it. Knowing the price is not the same as understanding the deal, though, and the show has yet to explain how the bargain was struck or what keeps it in force.

The fourth season's finale showed how little the residents understand about what the founders set in motion. Boyd and other townspeople uprooted the Bottle Tree, ComicBook.com reports, and a crimson lightning storm appeared over the town after the retrieval went wrong. The residents acted without knowing the rules behind the place, and they now face a storm none of them can explain. A question about the town's origins has become a pressing problem for the people living there now.

The residents still can't explain the bargain, so the way out may not lie in the children's story alone. The sacrifice now belongs to the larger struggle between the Man in Yellow and the Boy in White, and the final season may resolve it through that contest rather than as a separate puzzle.

For the ending, the real test is whether anyone breaks the bargain's hold on the town. A full account of how the founders struck it may matter less, and an ending built around the characters can deliver that kind of answer.

The show has already confirmed that people in Fromville come back, so the open question about reincarnation is no longer whether it happens but what it is for. FandomWire groups the question surrounding Tabitha and Jade with the town's origins and sacrifices. That grouping fits Tabitha, whom the previous season confirmed as a reincarnation of Miranda, according to Primetimer. Explaining the returns may also mean explaining the deaths that come with them, and whether the killings in the town follow a schedule.

The same family arrives in each cycle. Miranda came to town in the 1970s with her son, Victor, and her daughter, Eloise, Primetimer reports, and Tabitha arrived with one son and one daughter. Victor alone came through Miranda's cycle alive, and in an earlier cycle, the original Tabitha's daughter was sacrificed and became an Anghkooey child. In both cycles viewers know, a daughter was lost. If that pattern holds, Tabitha's daughter came into town already in danger, and the ending can't leave that unanswered.

FROM requires a person to die before being reborn, Primetimer writer Kinette Sumadia writes, and she uses that rule to argue that neither Victor nor the Boy in White is a reincarnation. By that reading, Victor never returned at all. He stayed. He also wants Ethan prepared to survive alone, which suggests he expects Miranda's outcome to repeat with Tabitha's family. Sumadia also connects Ethan to the Boy in White, a link the show hasn't confirmed.

The final season has to decide whether anyone can stop the cycle, because that determines whether Tabitha's children face the same split Miranda's did.

Fromville has a history of mass killing that nobody has accounted for, and that history may not be over. FandomWire lists the 1978 massacre as unexplained and says residents may face new purges each time they discover too much. That sets the logic of the show against its own characters. They stay alive by chasing answers, yet the more they uncover, the closer they may come to the next killing. The final season can't file the massacre away as backstory, because the residents face more danger with every mystery they solve.

The fourth season's finale has already shown what removing one resident looks like. Reel 360 reports that Sophia killed Elgin after he prayed, and his death reads as punishment for defying her rather than as bad luck. Sophia is the vessel for the Man in Yellow, so this killing leads straight to the show's central antagonist and not to a stray monster. She may also have been in town long before this season. The old photo Elgin found shows Sophia, which makes her a possible link between the deaths of the past and those of the present, even though nothing yet places her at the 1978 massacre.

The final season may never give the massacre a tidy explanation drawn from the past, and the writers could answer it with a fresh killing instead. Another round of deaths seems as likely a way to resolve the mystery as a scene set in 1978, though the show has committed to neither. The ending depends on this question because it decides whether the danger in Fromville comes from rules the characters can learn, or from one antagonist who punishes anyone who gets too close.

The bloodiest secret in Fromville may be settled not by finding out what happened in 1978, but by whether it happens again.

Two figures appear to stand outside the rules that trap everyone else in Fromville, and the show has explained neither of them. Nobody yet knows who the Man in Yellow and the Boy in White are or what either one wants, and the two differ sharply in how freely they act and how they help the residents, according to FandomWire. The Man in Yellow spent the fourth season disguised as Sophia, living among the residents without their knowing who he was, according to ScreenRant. The Boy in White, by contrast, offers only cryptic guidance, so the residents receive help they can't fully interpret. One of the two moves among the townspeople under a stolen identity, while the other stays at the edges of town.

The fourth season's finale turned these two separate puzzles into a single rivalry. Its final scene placed the Boy in White with Sophia just as Sophia was revealed as the Man in Yellow in disguise, ScreenRant reports, and the meeting was deliberate. Series creator and executive producer John Griffin told ScreenRant the scene was "confirmation that these two figures, who have operated in the background and whom we've only seen on the periphery, are absolutely aware of each other." From here on, any answer about what one of them is has to account for the other.

The difference in their freedom could reflect a choice or a limit, and the show hasn't said which. The Boy in White may keep his distance on purpose, or he may be unable to do more. ScreenRant reports that showrunner and executive producer Jeff Pinkner sees the story as taking shape as it goes rather than following a fixed course. The contest between the two figures is left for the final season to settle. The open question is also a practical one for the characters. How much help the residents can count on before the series ends depends on whether the Boy in White won't act or can't act.

Julie has walked away from the one power in town that lets a person move through the past, and nobody has ever explained how that power works. FandomWire lists her storywalking as unexplained, vanished or changed. ScreenRant reports that Julie gave up on storywalking in the fourth season's ninth episode. The show hasn't said whether the ability faded or changed or whether she simply stopped using it, so the open question now covers Julie as well as the power. With a single season left, the show has to bring the power back on purpose or knowingly let it go.

Whatever storywalking can do, it can't undo every loss in town. Julie can't reverse Jim's death, according to ScreenRant. That limit changes what is at stake each time she goes back. The power may matter more for what Julie could learn than for anything she could change. Casey Duby, a writer and critic at ScreenRant, suggests Julie could look into earlier cycles to expose the Man in Yellow's secrets. In a final season, knowing what happened before may be worth more to the survivors than any chance to erase it.

Julie's doubt now reaches beyond her own ability to the survivors' other plans for fighting back. She argued that Tabitha's plan was a trap, ScreenRant reports, and that suspicion now shapes whom the group is willing to believe. Her doubt places storywalking inside the struggle with the Man in Yellow. Julie has set aside a power that might show his past while the survivors argue over which guidance to follow.

If the ending explains what storywalking is and why Julie gave it up, it could explain a good deal about the Man in Yellow along the way.

In Fromville, characters who die do not necessarily leave the story. Residents the town has lost keep appearing again, from Father Khatri to Jim, and FandomWire counts these returns among the town's entities and supernatural powers, with no explanation for them confirmed. That places the visits closer to a force at work in the town than to grief or memory. Who sends the dead back is as open a question as who they are, and the answer matters more than any list of the names that reappear.

The fourth season's finale suggests the apparitions may belong to a larger mystery. Elgin found an old photo of a young woman who looked just like Sophia, and the finale then revealed that the Man in Yellow had been taking her form, according to Entertainment Weekly. At least one familiar face in town may therefore be borrowed from its past, and recognizing a face no longer proves who is behind it. If the Man in Yellow can take on the appearance of people from the town's history, the returning dead may be his instruments rather than ghosts acting on their own. In that case, the final season could settle the apparitions without addressing them directly, simply by explaining what the Man in Yellow is.

The ending still needs an answer, because the characters can no longer trust what they see. The figure behind Sophia's face can also cloud the minds of the living, and in the finale Sophia drugged Henry and left him hallucinating, according to Entertainment Weekly. Elgin, the resident who came closest to the truth about Sophia's face, died in the same finale, and he now stands among the dead whose possible return is an open question. Any sighting of him in the final season would raise the same doubt he was chasing. A vision in town can be false in two ways, with a dead face being worn or a living mind being clouded.

The finale does not need a catalog of every ghost. It does need a rule for when the dead are really themselves, because the characters cannot make their last choices without knowing whom to trust.

The residents' only proven weapon against a Creature works by rules the show has never explained. Boyd used blood worms to bring down the Smiley Creature, Primetimer reports, but what the worms and the blood did to Boyd and to Henry is still an open question, according to FandomWire. That makes the question a matter of survival, because the residents can't plan a final fight around a weapon whose rules none of them understand. Boyd can't simply strike again, either, because the worms were expelled from his body once he passed them on. Martin carried the worms before Boyd and died while transferring them, so the only man who might have said where they came from is gone. If the town is going to use the worms again, the last season may have to return to the question of where more of them could come from.

The steps of the transfer are known even though its workings are not. "My blood is your blood," Martin said as he handed the worms on, as reported by Primetimer, and Boyd repeated the vow almost word for word when he passed them to Smiley. Boyd copied what he had seen Martin do, but repeating the words is not the same as knowing why a Creature dies when the worms reach it. Until the show reveals those rules, any victory over the Creatures would look like luck rather than a payoff.

Fatima faces the same unknown from the other side, because nobody yet knows whether her change into something monstrous is permanent. The town's rules can destroy a Creature and can also remake a person, and the show has explained neither process.

The worms are a plot device, but Fatima is a character, and whether she can come back is the part of this mystery the final season can least afford to leave open.

Most of the town's open questions ask who or what is out there, but this one asks where. Nobody knows what part the lighthouse or the Lake of Tears plays in breaking the curse, or what would happen if someone brought bones to the lighthouse or the tower, according to FandomWire. That puts escape itself at stake. If the two places do figure in breaking the curse, the residents may be missing a step in the way out of town without knowing what that step does.

The lake entered the story in Ethan's dream in the first season's second episode, Primetimer reports, and for a long time afterward it remained only a dream image. The show never said what it was for, even as it added other mysteries around it. That changed in "Fray," the fourth season's second episode, when Ethan was given the job of finding the lake. The show still hasn't explained what the lake does, but a character is now searching for it on screen. The answer is therefore more likely to come through Ethan's search than through anyone's theories.

Ethan is not the only resident tied to the lake. Victor has a drawing of it in his room at the Colony House, according to Primetimer. Ethan dreamed of a place that already meant something to another person in town. How Victor came to know the lake has not been determined. The lake now holds two questions at once: what it is, and what Victor knows about it. In this last stretch of the show, several questions may share a single answer, and the lake and Victor may turn out to be one of them.

The two landmarks do not carry equal weight in the story. The lake has a dream, an errand and a second resident behind it, while the lighthouse is so far mainly the other half of a question about the curse. For an ending built around where the characters arrive, that difference matters. Ethan is already looking for the lake, so the finale would have a hard time leaving it unexplained. The lighthouse may get only as much explanation as the curse requires, which would make it the half of this mystery that fans keep arguing over longest.

Credit: Mjolnir47542 / MGM+ via From Wiki (Fandom)

Every night in Fromville, residents rely on talismans whose origins nobody can explain. The person or force that crafted the charms, and the source of their power, both lack an answer, according to FandomWire. ScreenRant movie and TV news editor Matthew Rudoy counts the makers and the power source as still unknown as well. Residents would have a hard time rebuilding or repairing a protection whose source they can't name. The charms' origin is therefore a stake in the ending and not a piece of trivia.

Part of the talisman's mystery has already been solved on screen. The meaning of the show's recurring symbols remains on the list of open questions, but ScreenRant reports that Tabitha realized in season 4 that the talisman symbol depicts her and Jade. That answer ties the town's protection to two of its current residents. It still doesn't say who put their image on the charms or why.

The question became urgent in the season 4 finale, when the Man in Yellow took the charms away. Disguised as Sophia, he removed talismans and threw them down a Faraway Tree, according to Reel 360 and ComicBook.com. It isn't clear whether she took most of them or all of them, so the final season also has to settle whether any protection is left. An enemy who went to the trouble of removing the charms likely saw them as a real obstacle, and that makes the question of who built them more pressing.

The red lightning looks like the wildest riddle here, but viewers saw when it began. FandomWire lists what sets it off as unanswered, while ScreenRant places its start right after the bottle tree was pulled up. The show hasn't explained how the tree and the storm are connected. The lightning was not the only upheaval, though. Pulling up the tree also turned the daylight to darkness and shook the ground, and Tabitha and Jade ended up stuck underground in the caves. The lightning is probably one part of that wider disruption, and the show may explain it along with the rest. Sophia reacted to the tree's removal with glee, a sign that worse may be coming for the residents.

The ending doesn't need to decode the lightning so much as account for it: who built the town's defenses, and what the red lightning means for the residents now that those defenses have been taken.

Fromville's daily workings remain as unexplained as its deepest secrets. The exits, the spiders, the music box storyline and the source of the town's electricity and supplies all stay murky, according to FandomWire. The explanations for the electricity and fuel that most Reddit $RDDT users accept are fan opinion, and the show has never confirmed them. Questions such as these rarely move the plot forward. Their answers still decide whether the town feels like a built world or an improvised one, and a final season that skips them risks the second.

Residents receive some necessities and not others, and nobody has explained why. Whatever keeps the town supplied has not brought medical care, because there are no doctors, hospitals or surgeons in town, according to TV Insider. That turns a logistics question into a story question. Any account of how the residents get their supplies would also have to explain why medical care is missing. Such an answer would say something about the nature of the place where these people are trapped.

What the town knows about the Faraway Trees begins with one man. Residents use the name Victor Kavanaugh gave the hollow-trunked bottle trees, and Victor is the town's longest-tenured resident, according to TV Insider. No one in town has seen more of the place than he has. The trees' secret is therefore partly a question of how much Victor knows and how much of it he shares with the others.

The trees may also reach back further than Victor. TV Insider reports that no connection between the trees and the town's former residents has been uncovered yet. If that link exists, the trees belong to the town's history as well as its geography, and to the larger story of who came before and what happened to them.

The power supply is the question the finale can most safely leave to fans, because it has no bearing on whether anyone can get out. The trees and the exits are a different matter. They bear on escape itself and on what the town's longest-tenured resident has kept to himself.

The last season needs to explain the trees, and it can leave the electricity a mystery.

Lady Gaga Hits a Yoga Class in Slingback Heels

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

You will rarely catch her in a pair of flats

These days, many seem to be steering away from high heels and challenging the Carrie Bradshaw mindset, opting instead for comfier silhouettes, like ballet flats or opera shoes. Even heels themselves are getting more sensible: think kitten heels rather than teetering stilettos. But Lady Gaga rarely swims with the current.

Since the dawn of her career, the Grammy-winning pop star has worn shoes that seem to defy the laws of physics. She hits the streets in heel-less Nortek Tatehana pointe shoes and sky-high leather Pleasers. Her “Bad Romance” music video helped solidify Alexander McQueen’s scythe-shaped Armadillo shoes as an accessory icon. Sometimes, her heels are even covered in meat. So, it should come as no surprise that, in Gaga’s world, even a workout class calls for a fresh pair of heels.

On Tuesday afternoon, the “Abracadabra” singer was seen heading into a yoga class in Mill Valley, California, while in an easy all-black fit composed of your standard workout fare: a baggy T-shirt and joggers paired with a black baseball cap, plus a scrunchie on her wrist to help pull back her silvery hair. But in true Gaga fashion, she traded workout shoes for a pair of pointy black slingbacks, which gave the casual athleisure look a chic, witchy effect.

This is one of the first times that Gaga has been spotted in public since she and her fiancé, Michael Polansky, welcomed their first child together in June. And while Gaga’s life (and sleep schedule) has surely changed quite dramatically in these past few months, clearly her fun approach to style has not.
